Vector Spaces (Chapter 1)
Rn and Cn (Section 1A)
Lists (Sub-Section 1A1)
Def 1.8 (list, length):
A list of length n is the same as an n tuple. We write a list of vectors of length m (v1 , . . . , vm ) as
v1 , . . . , vm throughout this document without closing parentheses. Since lists are the same as tuples,
two lists are only equal if they have the same length and the same elements in the same order.
Def 1.11 (F n , coordinate):



F n := x1 , . . . , xn : xk ∈ F , k ∈ {1, . . . , n}

Definition of a Vector Space (Section 1B)
Def 1.20 (vector space):
A “vector space” is a set V along with an addition on V and a scalar multiplication on V such that the
following properties hold.
• Commutativity: u + v = v + u

∀u, v ∈ V

• Associativity: (u + v) + w = u + (v + w) and (ab)v = a(bv)
• Additive identity: ∃0 ∈ V : v + 0 = v

∀u, v, w ∈ V and ∀a, b ∈ F

∀v ∈ V .

∃! is not a requirement, but a property of vector spaces. See 1.26.
• Additive inverse: ∀v ∈ V ,

∃w ∈ V : v + w = 0

• Multiplicative identity: 1v = v

∀v ∈ V

• Distributive properties: a(u + v) = au + av

∀u, v ∈ V and

(a + b)v = av + bv

∀a, b ∈ F and v ∈ V .

Def 1.24 (notation F S ):
If S is a set, then F S denotes the set of functions from S to F . Let f , g ∈ F S and λ ∈ F :
• The “sum” f + g ∈ F S is defined as follows: (f + g)(x) := f (x) + g(x)
• The “product” λf ∈ F S is defined as follows: (λf )(x) := λf (x)

∀x ∈ S.

∀x ∈ S.

The vector space F n :≡ F {1,2,...,n} is a special case, because each (x1 , . . . , xn ) ∈ F n can be thought of
as a function x from the set {1, 2, . . . , n} to F by writing x(k) instead of xk for the k th coordinate of
(x1 , . . . , xn ).
Thm 1.26 (unique additive identity):
A vector space has a unique additive identity.
Proof: Suppose 0 and 0′ are both additive identities. Then 0′ = 0′ + 0 = 0 + 0′ = 0. So, 0′ = 0.
Thm 1.27 (unique additive inverse):
Every element in a vector space has a unique additive inverse.
Proof: Let v ∈ V . Suppose u, w ∈ V are additive inverses of v. Using associativity and the definition
of the additive inverse, we get
u = u + 0 = u + (v + w) = (u + v) + w = 0 + w = w.
Thus, u = w, as desired.

Definition of a Vector Space (Section 1B)

Def 1.28:
For v, w ∈ V , −v denotes the additive inverse of v, and we define subtraction as w − v := w + (−v).
Def 1.29:
For the rest of this summary, V denotes a vector space over F most of the time.
Thm 1.30 (the number 0 times a vector):
0v = 0

∀v ∈ V .

Proof: Let v ∈ V . Thus, 0v = (0 + 0)v = 0v + 0, which implies 0 = 0v. Here we have been using the
definition of additive identity and the distributive property. After that, we subtract 0v on both
sides of the equation.
Thm 1.31 (a number times the vector 0):
a⃗0 = ⃗0 ∀a ∈ F .
Proof: For every a ∈ F , we have a⃗0 = a(⃗0 + ⃗0) = a⃗0 + a⃗0. This implies ⃗0 = a⃗0. (Using the definition of
additive identity (⃗0 + ⃗0 = ⃗0) and one of the distributive properties. After that, we subtract ⃗0a on
both sides of the equation).
Thm 1.32 (a number −1 times a vector):
(−1)v = −v

∀v ∈ V .

Proof: Let v ∈ V . Hence, v + (−1)v = 1v + (−1)v = (1 + (−1))v = 0v = 0, using the distributive property
and the definition of the multiplicative identity. This equation (v + (−1)v = 0) tells us that (−1)v
is the additive inverse of v, and therefore, (−1)v = −v.

Exercises about Definition of a Vector Space 1B
1

Prove that −(−v) = v

∀v ∈ V .

Proof: Let v ∈ V . By definition, we know that v + (−v) = 0. We also know by definition, that the
additive inverse of (−v), which is unique by 1.26, is denoted by −(−v). The very first equations
shows that −(−v) = v, since v + (−v) = −(−v) + (−v) = 0.

Suppose v, w ∈ V . Explain why there exists a unique x ∈ V such that v + 3x = w.
Solution: To solve this equation for x, we first need to subtract v from w and aftwewards

Subspaces (Section 1C)
Def 1.33 (subspace):
A subset U of V (U ⊆ V ) is called a “subspace” of V if U is also a vector space with the same additive
identity, addition, and scalar multiplication as on V .
Thm 1.34 (conditions for a subspace):
A subset U of V is a subspace of V ⇐⇒ U satisfies the following three conditions:
• Additive identity: 0 ∈ U
• Closed under addition: u, w ∈ U =⇒ u + w ∈ U
• Closed under scalar multiplication: a ∈ F and u ∈ U =⇒ au ∈ U
The additive identity condition above could be replaced by the condition that U is nonempty (because then taking u ∈ U and multiplying it by 0 would imply that 0 ∈ U ).
Proof: We need to show the proposition in two directions.
⇒ direction:

If U is a subspace of V , then U satisfies the three conditions above by the

definition of a vector space.
⇐ direction:

The first condition ensures that the additive identity 0 of U is in U . The

other two conditions make sure that addition and scalar multiplication make sense on U .
Thanks to 1.32 and the third condition (closedness under scalar multiplication), every u ∈
U has an additive inverse −u = (−1)u. The other parts of the definition of a vector space,
such as associativity, commutativity, and the distributive properties, are automatically
satisfied for U because they hold on the larger space V . Thus, U is a vector space and
hence a subspace of V .

Sum of Subspaces (Sub-Section 1C1)
Def 1.36 (sum of subspaces):
If V1 , . . . , Vm are subspaces of V , the “sum” of V1 , . . . , Vm , denoted by V1 + · · · + Vm , is the set of all
possible sums of elements of V1 , . . . , Vm . More precisely,
V1 + · · · + Vm := {v1 + · · · + vm | v1 ∈ V1 , . . . , vm ∈ Vm }.
Thm 1.40 (sum of subspaces is the smallest containing subspace):
If V1 , . . . , Vm are subspaces of V , then V1 +· · ·+Vm is the smallest subspace of V containing V1 , . . . , Vm .

Direct Sums (Sub-Section 1C2)
Def 1.41 (direct sum, ⊕):
V1 + · · · + Vm is called a “direct sum” if each element of V1 + · · · + Vm can be written in only one way as
a sum v1 + · · · + vm , where each vk ∈ Vk . In this case, we write
V1 + · · · + V m = V 1 ⊕ · · · ⊕ V m
to denote that V1 + · · · + Vm is a direct sum.

Thm 1.46 (sum and intersection of two subspaces):
U + W is a direct sum, i.e., U + W = U ⊕ W ⇐⇒ U ∩ W = {0}.
Proof: Let U and W be subspaces of the same vector space.
⇒ direction: First, suppose U + W is a direct sum. If v ∈ U ∩ W , then v, −v ∈ U and v, −v ∈
W . We also have that
0 = v + (−v), where v ∈ U and (−v) ∈ W .
Because U + W is a direct sum and writing 0 as 0 = 0 + (−0) = 0 + 0 is unique, the equation
above implies that v = 0. Therefore, U ∩ W = {0}.
⇐ direction: Suppose U ∩ W = {0}. Let u ∈ U and w ∈ W such that
0 = u + w.

(1.1)

By 1.45, we know that our goal is to show that this implies u = w = 0. The equation (1.1)
above implies that u = −w ∈ W and w = −u ∈ U for all arbitrary choices of U and W . Thus,
u, w ∈ U ∩ W . Hence, u = w = 0. Therefore, U + W is a direct sum.

Exercises about Subspaces 1C
1

For each of the following subsets of F 3 , determine wether is is a subspace of F 3 .



(a) x1 , x2 , x3 ∈ F : x1 + 2x2 + 3x3 = 0
5

Subspaces (Section 1C)




(b) x1 , x2 , x3 ∈ F : x1 + 2x2 + 3x3 = 4



(c) x1 , x2 , x3 ∈ F : x1 x2 x3 = 0



(d) x1 , x2 , x3 ∈ F : x1 = 5x3
Solution:
(a) Let E :≡ {(x1 , x2 , x3 ) ∈ F | x1 +2x2 +3x3 = 0}, then E is a subspace of F 3 because it is a plane.
We have the additive identity 0 ∈ {(x1 , x2 , x3 ) ∈ F | x1 + 2x2 + 3x3 = 0}. For u, v ∈ E with their
coordinates satisfying u1 + 2u2 + 3u3 = 0 and v1 + 2v2 + 3v3 = 0, we also have the condition
that (u1 + v1 ) + 2(u2 + v2 ) + 3(u3 + v3 ) = 0. So u + v ∈ E. Hence E is closed under addition. If
w ∈ E and λ ∈ F , then if u1 + 2u2 + 3u3 = 0, it also holds that λu1 + 2λu2 + 3λu3 = 0. Hence
λu ∈ E. So E is closed under scalar multiplication. Therefore, E is a subspace of F 3 by
1.34.
(b) It is not a subspace because is does not contain 0. It is also not closed under addtition and
scalar multiplication.
(c) Let U :≡ {(x1 , x2 , x3 ) ∈ F | x1 x2 x3 = 0}. Then U is actually the union of the xy-, the xz- and
the yz-plane, because its description tells us that at least one of its coordinates has to be
zero. Thus we have
(1, 1, 0) + (0, 1, 1) = (1, 2, 1) ∈/ U .
Therefore, U is not closed under addition. But it is closed under scalar multiplication and
we also have 0 ∈ U . Therefore, U is not a subspace of F 3 .
(d) Let U :≡ {(x1 , x2 , x3 ) ∈ F | x1 = 5x3 }. Then 0 ∈ U , so we have checked for additive identity.
Let u, v ∈ U . Then u + v = (5u3 + 5v3 , u2 + v2 , u3 + v3 ) = (5(u3 + v3 ), u2 + v2 , u3 + v3 ) ∈ U , so its
closed under addition. Simmilarly for λ ∈ F and w ∈ U we have λu = (λ5u3 , λu2 , λu3 ) =
(5λu3 , λu2 , λu3 ) ∈ U , so U is a subspace of F 3 .

Verify all assertions about subspaces in example 1.35.
(a) If b ∈ F then U :≡ {(x1 , x2 , x3 , x4 ) ∈ F | x3 = 5x4 + b} is a subspace of F 4 ⇐⇒ b = 0.
Solution: If v, u ∈ U , then
v + u = (v1 + u1 , v2 + u2 , v3 + u3 , v4 + u4 , v5 + u4 )
= (v1 + u1 , v2 + u2 , v3 + u3 , 5v4 + b + 5u4 + b, v5 + u4 )
= (v1 + u1 , v2 + u2 , v3 + u3 , 5(v4 + u4 ) + 2b, v5 + u4 )
Only if b = 0, it can hold that the third coordinate of v + u is 5 times the fourth coordinate
of v + u plus an element of F acting as b. Also note that if λ ∈ F and w ∈ U , then λw =
(λw1 , λw2 , λu4 + λb, λu4 ). In this case, we have λw ∈ U if and only if b = 0.
(b) The set of continious real-valued functions on the intervall [0, 1] is a subspace of R[0,1]
(The set of real valued functions on the invervall [0, 1])
Solution: The constant function 0 is continious. Adding two continious functions together yields a continious function. Multiplying a continious function with a scalar also
gives us a continious function.
(c) The set of differentiable real-valued functions of R is a subspace of RR .

∀x ∈ R is also contained.

(d) The set S of differentiable real-valued functions f on the inverfall (0, 3) such that f ′ (2) = b
is a subspace of R(0,3) if and only if b = 0.

3

Show that the set S of differentiable real-valued functions f on the invervall (−4, 4) such that
f ′ (−1) = 3f (2) is a subspace of R(−4,4) .
Solution: We check for all three properties.
Additive identity: For f (x) :≡ 0, f ′ (x) = 0 we have f ′ (−1) = 0 = 3 · 0 = 3f (2), so f ′ belongs so S
as well.
Closed under addition: For h(x) :≡ f (x) + g(x), h′ (x) = f ′ (x) + g ′ (x) we got h′ (−1) = f ′ (−1) +
g ′ (−1) = 3f (2) + 3g(2) = 3(f (2) + g(2)) = 3h(2). Therefore, h ∈ S.
Closed under scalar multiplication: For h(x) :≡ cf (x), h′ (x) = cf ′ (x) we got h′ (−1) = cf ′ (−1) =
c3f (2) = 3cf (2) = 3h(x). So if h′ ∈ S whenever h ∈ S.
Hence S is a subspace of R(−4,4) .

Prove or give a counterexample: If U is a nonempty subset of R2 such that U is closed under
addition and under taking additive inverses (meaning −u ∈ U whenever u ∈ U ), then U is
subspace of R2 .
Solution: This statement is wrong. Take U :≡ {(x, y) ∈ R2 | (x, y) ∈ Z2 } ⊂ R2 . Then U is closed
under addtion since Z is closed under addition. Example: (2, 1) + (−2, 1) + (1, 1) = (1, 1) ∈ U . But
if λ ∈ R such that λ ∈/ Z we have for a, b ∈ Z:
λ(a, b) = (λa, λb) ∈/ U .
For example: π(3, 4) ∈/ U . Therefore U is not a subspace of R2 .

Span and Linear Independence (Section 2A)
Linear Combinations and Span (Sub-Section 2A1)
Def 2.2 (linear combination):
a1 v1 + · · · + am vm is called a “linear combination” of a list of vectors v1 , . . . , vm (a1 , . . . , am ∈ F ).
Def 2.4 (span of a list of vectors):
The “span” of a list of vectors v1 , . . . , vm is defined as follows:
span(v1 , . . . , vm ) :≡ {a1 v1 + · · · + am vm | a1 , . . . , am ∈ F }
span( ) :≡ {0} (The span of the empty list ( ) is defined to be {0})
The definition also implies that span(0) = {0}, or written differently, span(⃗0) = {⃗0}. Note that sometimes we write ⃗0 and sometimes we write 0 throughout this document. Later on, 0 sometimes also
means the zero-operator.
Thm 2.6 (the span of a list of vectors is a subspace):
The span of a list of vectors in V is the smallest subspace of V containing all vectors in the list.
Proof: Let v1 , . . . , vm ∈ V . We have
0 = 0v1 + · · · + 0vm ∈ span(v1 , . . . , vm ),
and hence, the additive identity is in span(v1 , . . . , vm ).
span(v1 , . . . , vm ) is closed under addition, because for all a’s and c’s in F , we have
(a1 v1 + · · · + am vm ) + (c1 v1 + · · · + cm vm ) = (a1 + c1 )v1 + · · · + (am + cm )vm ∈ span(v1 , . . . , vm ).
|

} |
{z
}
∈ span(v1 , ... ,vm )

∈ span(v1 , ... ,vm )

Furthermore, span(v1 , . . . , vm ) is closed under scalar multiplication because for λ ∈ R, we have:
λ (a1 v1 + · · · + am vm ) = λa1 v1 + · · · + λam vm ∈ span(v1 , . . . , vm ).
|

}
∈ span(v1 , ... ,vm )

Thus, span(v1 , . . . , vm ) is a subspace of V by 1.34. Every subspace of V that contains each vk
is closed under scalar multiplication and addition, and therefore, contains span(v1 , . . . , vm ).
Hence, span(v1 , . . . , vm ) is the smallest subspace containing each v1 , . . . , vm . The fact that
vj ∈ span(v1 , . . . , vm )

(j = 1, . . . , m)

is obvious.
Def 2.7 (spanning a vector space):
If V = span(v1 , . . . , vm ), we say v1 , . . . , vm “spans” V .
Def 2.9 (finite-dimensional vector space):
Such a vector space is called finite-dimensional. We write
dim V ̸= ∞ or dim V < ∞.
Def 2.10 (polynomial, P (F )):
A function p : F → F is called a “polynomial” with coefficients in F if there exist a0 , . . . , am ∈ F such
that
p(z) = a0 + a1 z + a2 z2 + · · · + am zm

∀z ∈ F .
9

Span and Linear Independence (Section 2A)

We further denote
• P (F ) as the set of all polynomials with coefficients in F .
• Note that P (F ) is a vector space over F and a subspace of F F .
Def 2.11 (degree of a polynomial, deg p):
The degree m of a polynomial p = a0 + a1 z + a2 z2 + · · · + am zm is denoted with
deg p := m.
We also define for the zero polynomial p = 0:
deg p = deg 0 := −∞.
Def 2.12 (the set of all polynomials with degree m):
Pm (F ) = span(1, z, . . . , zm ) denotes the set of all polynomials with coefficients in F and degree at most
m. One should actually use the lambda notation λz.zm to speak of functions and write Pm (F ) =
span(λz.1, λz.z, . . . , λz.zm ), but this is not done most of the time. Note that
dim Pm (F ) = m + 1.
Def 2.13 (infinite-dimensional):
A vector space is called “infinite-dimensional” if it is not finite-dimensional.

Linear Independence (Sub-Section 2A2)
Def 2.15 (linear independence):
There are 2 ways to think about “linear independence”. Let’s first start with the motivation. A list of
vectors v1 , . . . , vm is called “linearly independent” if every combination of these vectors w = b1 v1 + · · · +
bm vm ∈ span(v1 , . . . , vm ) has a unique choice of scalars b1 , . . . , bm ∈ F . That means there does not exist

another choice b1′ , . . . , bm

The actual definition of linear independence:
A list of vectors v1 , . . . , vm is called linearly independentif the only way to combine them together and
yield zero is if we choose every coefficient λi to be zero:
λ1 v1 + · · · + λm vm = 0 ⇐⇒ λ1 = · · · = λm = 0.
Another way to put it:
Let w ∈ span(v1 , . . . , vm ) such that for a1 , . . . , am , c1 , . . . , cm ∈ F :
w = a1 v1 + · · · + am vm and
w = c1 v1 + · · · + cm vm
⇐⇒
0 = (a1 − c1 ) v1 + · · · + (am − cm ) vm .

If a1 = c1 , a2 = c2 , . . . , am = cm is the only solution, the representation of w as a linear combination of
v1 , . . . , vm is unique and the only way to add them up together equaling 0 is 0v1 + · · · + 0vm = 0. Both
of these definitions are equivalent.
Note: The empty list () is also defined to be linearly independent.
Def 2.16 (linear dependence):
Otherwise, v1 , . . . , vm is called “linearly dependent”. For example, let V be a 2-dimensional vector

space and let u, v ∈ V be linearly dependent. Then we have a, b such that au + bv = 0 with a ̸= 0 and
b ̸= 0. Thus, we can write: u = (−b/a) v.

Thm 2.22 (length of linearly independent list ≤ length of spanning list):
In a finite-dimensionalvector space, the length of every linearly independent list of vectors is less
than or equal to the length of every spanning list of vectors.
Proof: Let u1 , . . . , um ∈ V be a linearly independent list and let w1 , . . . , wn ∈ V such that
V = span(w1 , . . . , wn ).

Thm 2.25 (finite-dimensional subspace):
Every subspace of a finite-dimensional vector space is finite-dimensional.
Proof: Suppose dim V < ∞, and U ⊆ V . Let m := dim V .
Step 1: If
U = {0},
we are done. Otherwise, pick any nonzero u1 ∈ U .
Step k, (2 ≤ k ≤ m): If
U = span(u1 , . . . , uk−1 ),
then U is finite-dimensionaland we are done.
If
U ̸= span(u1 , . . . , uk−1 ),
then choose a vector uk ∈ U such that
uk ∈/ span(u1 , . . . , uk−1 ).
After each step, as long as the process continues, we have constructed a list of vectors such that
no vector in the list is in the span of the previous vectors. Hence, at each stage we maintain
a linearly independent list (by the Linear Dependence Lemma (2.19)). No list of independent
vectors in V can exceed the length of any spanning list of V (by 2.22). Thus, the process
eventually terminates (after at most m = dim V steps), so U has a finite basis.

20 Suppose p0 , p1 , . . . , pm are polynomials in Pm (F ) such that pk (2) = 0 for 0 ≤ k ≤ m.
Prove that p0 , p1 , . . . , pm is not linearly independent in Pm (F ).
Proof: Define an evaluation map ϕ : Pm (F ) → F such that
ϕ(p) := p(2)

∀p ∈ P (F ).

The polynomials that vanish at z = 2 lie in the subspace null(ϕ) of Pm (F ). So, we have
null(ϕ) ⊆ Pm (F ).
Any polynomial p ∈ null(ϕ) can be written as
p(z) = (z − 2)q(z),
where q(z) ∈ Pm−1 (F ). Therefore, dim null(ϕ) = m. Since p0 , p1 , . . . , pm ∈ null(ϕ), the given list is
not linearly independent by 2.22, because it has length m + 1.

Bases (Section 2B)
Def 2.26 (basis):
A “basis” β of V is a list of vectors β = v1 , . . . , vn in V that is linearly independent and spans V .
V = span(v1 , . . . , vn ) = span(β)
Thm 2.28 (condition for a basis):
v1 , . . . , vn is a basis of V ⇐⇒ every v ∈ V can be written uniquely in the form
v = a1 v1 + · · · + an vn , where a1 , . . . , an ∈ F .
Note that the actual definition of linear dependence has said nothing about uniqueness.
Thm 2.30 (every spanning list contains a basis):
Every spanning list v1 , . . . , vn of a vector space V can be reduced to a basis of V . So, if V = span(v1 , . . . , vn ),
then we can delete elements from that list, or leave the list unchanged, such that the list forms a basis.
Proof: Suppose V = span(v1 , . . . , vn ). Let B0 :≡ (v1 , . . . , vn ).
Step 1: If v1 = 0, delete v1 from B0 :
Therefore, let B1 :≡ (v2 , . . . , vn ).
If v1 ̸= 0, then leave B0 unchanged:
B1 :≡ B0 = (v1 , . . . , vn ).

Stop the process after step n, getting a list Bn . The list Bn spans V because our original list B0
spanned V , and we have discarded only vectors that were already in the span of the previous
vectors. The process ensures that no vector in Bn is in the span of the previous ones. Thus, Bn
is linearly independent. Hence, Bn is a basis of V .
Thm 2.31 (basis of finite-dimensional vector space):
Every finite-dimensional vector space has a basis.
Proof: By definition, a finite-dimensional vector space has a spanning list. The previous result tells
us that each spanning list can be reduced to a basis.
Thm 2.32:
Every linearly independentlist of vectors in a finite-dimensional vector space can be extended to a
basis of the vector space.
Proof: Let u1 , . . . , um ∈ V be linearly independent. Let V = span(w1 , . . . , wn ).
=⇒ V = span(u1 , . . . , um , w1 , . . . , wn ).
Applying the procedure of the proof of 2.30 to reduce this list to a basis of V produces a basis
consisting of vectors u1 , . . . , um and some w’s.

Bases (Section 2B)

Thm 2.33 (every subspace of V is part of a direct sum equal to V ):
Suppose V is finite-dimensional and U is a subspace of V . Then there is a subspace W of V such that
V = U ⊕W.
Note: One may choose W = V or W = {0}.
Proof: Suppose U is a subspace of a finite-dimensional vector space V . Therefore, by 2.25, U is also
finite-dimensional. Hence, there exist vectors u1 , . . . , um ∈ U such that u1 , . . . , um form a basis of
U by 2.31. We may extend u1 , . . . , um to a basis u1 , . . . , um , w1 , . . . , wn of V , using 2.32. Set
W := span(w1 , . . . , wn ).
By 1.46, to show that V = U ⊕ W , it suffices to prove that V = U + W and U ∩ W = {0}.
Proof of V = U + W : Let v ∈ V . Since u1 , . . . , um , w1 , . . . , wn is a basis of V , we may write
v = (a1 u1 + · · · + am um ) + (b1 w1 + · · · + bn wn )
|
{z
} |
{z
}
u

w

for some scalars a1 , . . . , am , b1 , . . . , bn ∈ F . With u and w defined as above, we have v = u + w,
where u ∈ U and w ∈ W . Hence, v ∈ U +W , and so V ⊆ U +W . Conversely, since U , W ⊆ V ,
we have U + W ⊆ V . Therefore, V = U + W .
Proof of U ∩W = {0}: Let v ∈ U ∩W . Then, there exist scalars a1 , . . . , am , b1 , . . . , bn ∈ F such that
v = a1 u1 + · · · + am um and v = b1 w1 + · · · + bn wn . Thus, a1 u1 + · · · + am um − b1 w1 − · · · − bn wn = 0.
Linear independence of u1 , . . . , um , w1 , . . . , wn forces all ai = 0 and bj = 0. Therefore, v must
be zero, and U ∩ W = {0}.
Hence, V = U ⊕ W , as desired.

Claim: If v1 , v2 , v3 , v4 is a basis of V , then
v1 + v2 , v2 + v3 , v3 + v4 , v4 is also a basis of V .
Proof: Step 1: We first show linear independence. Let a1 , a2 , a3 , a4 ∈ F such that
a1 (v1 + v2 ) + a2 (v2 + v3 ) + a3 (v3 + v4 ) + a4 v4 = 0.
This is the case if and only if
a1 v1 + a1 v2 + a2 v2 + a2 v3 + a3 v3 + a3 v4 + a4 v4 = a1 v1 + (a1 + a2 )v2 + (a2 + a3 )v3 + (a3 + a4 )v4 = 0
Since v1 , . . . , v4 are linearly independent, this is only the case if 0 = a1 = a1 + a2 = a2 + a3 = a3 + a4
and therefore, since a1 = 0, ⇐⇒ a1 = · · · = a4 = 0. So the list v1 + v2 , v2 + v3 , v3 + v4 , v4 is linearly
independent as well.
Step 2: Now we also have to show, that v1 + v2 , v2 + v3 , v3 + v4 , v4 spans V . Let u ∈ V . With our
old basis, u would have been written as
u = b1 v1 + · · · + b4 v4 .

(2.7)

So let v ∈ V such that
v = a1 (v1 + v2 ) + a2 (v2 + v3 ) + a3 (v3 + v4 ) + a4 v4
= a1 v1 + (a1 + a2 )v2 + (a2 + a3 )v3 + (a3 + a4 )v4 = 0
Comparing this with the equation of u (2.7), we see that v can be indeed any vector in V , since
v1 , v2 , v3 , v4 is a basis.
If we want to rewrite vector u we would write a1 = b1 . Next we would have a1 + a2 = b2 and
therefore a2 = b2 −a1 = b2 −b1 . Next we would have a2 +a3 = b3 and thus a3 = b3 −a2 = b3 −(b2 −b1 ).
16

Bases (Section 2B)

Lastly a4 = b4 − a3 = b4 − (b3 − (b2 − b1 )). Putting this all together we have
u = b1 · (v1 + v2 ) + (b2 − b1 ) · (v2 + v3 ) + (b3 − (b2 − b1 )) · (v3 + v4 ) + (b4 − (b3 − (b2 − b1 ))) · v4
= b1 (v1 + v2 ) + (b2 − b1 )(v2 + v3 ) + (b3 − b2 + b1 )(v3 + v4 ) + (b4 − b3 + b2 − b1 )v4
= b1 v1 + b2 v2 + b3 v3 + b4 v4 .
which can be verified by comparing the coefficents of the v’s. For example, adding the two
coefficients (b3 − b2 + b1 ) and (b4 − b3 + b2 − b1 ) of v4 together yields b4 . So every u ∈ V can be
expressed in either of the two lists v1 , v2 , v3 , v4 or v1 + v2 , v2 + v3 , v3 + v4 , v4 . Since we have shown
linear independence as well, v1 + v2 , v2 + v3 , v3 + v4 , v4 is a basis of V .

Claim: If v1 , v2 , v3 , v4 is a basis of V and U ⊆ V , such that v1 , v2 ∈ U and v3 ∈/ U and v4 ∈/ U , then
v1 , v2 is a basis of U .
Proof: Any vector in U can be represented as a unique linear combination of v1 , v2 , v3 , v4 since
U ⊆ V . Let u ∈ U such that u = a1 v1 + a2 v2 + a3 v3 + a4 v4 . In this case, a3 must equal to 0,
because otherwise, due to closedness under addition and multiplication, we could subtract
a1 v1 + a2 v2 + a4 v4 from u and multiply by a1 to get v3 , which is not in U . The same goes for a4 ,
3

which must be 0 as well. So every vector in U is of the form u = a1 v1 + a2 v2 , since v1 , v2 ∈ U and
v3 , v4 ∈/ U . Since every basis has the same length and v1 and v2 are linearly independent, the
list v1 , v2 forms a basis of U .

Dimension (Section 2C)

Dimension (Section 2C)
Thm 2.34 (basis length):
Any two bases of a finite-dimensional vector space have the same length. So, the basis length does
not depend on the basis.
Proof: Let B1 and B2 be 2 bases of V . Since both are linearly independent and both span V , we have
|B1 |≤ |B2 | and |B2 |≤ |B1 | by using 2.22. Therefore, |B1 |= |B2 |.
Def 2.35 (dimension):
dim V :≡ length of any basis of V .
Thm 2.37 (dimension of a subspace):
If V is finite-dimensionaland U is a subspace of V , then
dim U ≤ dim V .
Proof: Since the length of every linearly independent list is smaller or equal to every spanning list
in V according to 2.22, we have the situation that the length of the basis of U is less than or
equal to the length of the basis of V .
Thm 2.38 (linearly independent list of the right length):
Every linearly independent list of vectors of length dim V is a basis.
Proof: Let dim V = n and v1 , . . . , vn be linearly independent in V . This list can be extended to a basis
of V by 2.32. However, every basis of V has length n, so no elements are adjoined to v1 , . . . , vn
by such an extension process.
Thm 2.39 (subspace of full dimension):
If U is a subspace of V and dim U = dim V ̸= ∞, then U = V .
Proof: Let u1 , . . . , un be a basis of U . Thus, n = dim U , and by the hypothesis, we also have n = dim V .
Thus, u1 , . . . , un is a linearly independent list of vectors in V of length dim V . So, by our previous
theorem 2.38, we see that u1 , . . . , un is a basis of V . In particular, every vector in V is a linear
combination of u1 , . . . , un . Thus, U = V .
Thm 2.42 (spanning list of the right length):
Every spanning list of vectors in V (dim V ̸= ∞) with length dim V is a basis of V .
Proof: Suppose dim V = n and v1 , . . . , vn spans V . The list v1 , . . . , vn can be reduced to a basis of V by
2.30. But our list already has length n, so no elements will get deleted. Thus, v1 , . . . , vn is a basis
of V , as desired.
Thm 2.43 (dimension of a sum):
If V1 and V2 are subspaces of a finite-dimensional vector space V , then
dim(V1 + V2 ) = dim V1 + dim V2 − dim(V1 ∩ V2 ).
Proof: Let r1 , . . . , rn ∈ V be a basis of V1 ∩ V2 , where n ∈ N≥0 . This means the basis can also be
the empty list, and V1 ∩ V2 might equal {⃗0}. Note that the empty list is defined to be linearly
independent, and the span of the empty list is defined to be equal to {⃗0}. By this definition, we
have
dim(V1 ∩ V2 ) = n.
Since r1 , . . . , rn is linearly independent in V as well as in V1 and V2 , this list can be extended to
18

Dimension (Section 2C)

a basis of V1 and V2 . So let
• r1 , . . . , rn , u1 , . . . , uj be a basis of V1 , and
• r1 , . . . , rn , w1 , . . . , wk be a basis of V2 for j, k ∈ N≥0 .
Then we have dim(V1 ) = n + j and dim(V2 ) = n + k. Note that the extensions might be empty
(j, k ∈ N≥0 ), meaning V1 or V2 could coincide with V1 ∩ V2 . If both subspaces equal V1 ∩ V2 ,
this would imply V1 = V2 . The reader can easily verify that the formula still holds in this case:
dim(V2 + V2 ) = dim(V2 ) = 2 · dim(V2 ) − dim(V2 ∩ V2 ).
We will show that
(2.8)

r1 , . . . , rn , u1 , . . . , uj , w1 , . . . , wk
is a basis of V1 + V2 . This will complete the proof because then we will have
dim(V1 + V2 ) = n + j + k
= (n + j) + (n + k) − n
= dim(V1 ) + dim(V2 ) − dim(V1 ∩ V2 ).

The list (2.8) is contained in V1 +V2 . The span of this list contains V1 and V2 ; therefore, it equals
V1 + V2 . Thus, to show that (2.8) is a basis of V1 + V2 , we only need to show that it is linearly
independent. Therefore, let a1 , . . . , an , b1 , . . . , bj , c1 , . . . , ck ∈ F , such that
0 = a1 r1 + · · · + an rn + b1 u1 + · · · + bj uj + c1 w1 + · · · + ck wk .

(2.9)

We need to prove that all the a’s, b’s, and c’s equal 0. The equation above can be rewritten as
c1 w1 + · · · + ck wk = −a1 r1 − · · · − an rn − b1 u1 − · · · − bj uj ,
which shows that c1 w1 + · · · + ck wk ∈ V1 because r1 , . . . , rn , u1 , . . . , uj is a basis of V1 . But all the

But r1 , . . . , rn , w1 , . . . , wk is linearly independent, because it is a basis of V2 , and the equation
above can be rewritten as 0 = c1 w1 + · · · + ck wk − d1 r1 − · · · − dn rn , so all the c’s and d’s must equal
0. Thus, (2.9) becomes the equation
a1 r1 + · · · + an rn + b1 u1 + · · · + bj uj = 0.
Because r1 , . . . , rn , u1 , . . . , uj is linearly independent, the equation above implies that all the a’s
and b’s are 0. So we have shown that in equation (2.9) all coefficients (a, b, c) are 0. In symbols:
a1 = 0, . . . , an = 0, b1 = 0, . . . , bj = 0, c1 = 0, . . . , ck = 0, completing the proof.

Exercises about Dimension 2C (Sub-Section 2C0)
20 Explain why you might guess, motivated by analogy with the formula for the number of elements in the union of three finite sets, that if V1 , V2 , V3 are subspaces of a finite-dimensional
vector space V , then
dim(V1 + V2 + V3 ) = dim(V1 ) + dim(V2 ) + dim(V3 )
− dim(V1 ∩ V2 ) − dim(V1 ∩ V3 ) − dim(V2 ∩ V3 )
+ dim(V1 ∩ V2 ∩ V3 ).
Then either prove the formula above or give a counterexample.
Solution: The formula is wrong. Consider the following subspaces of R2 :
19

Dimension (Section 2C)

• V1 := {(x, 0) | x ∈ R}

(the x-axis)

• V2 := {(x, x) | x ∈ R}

(the line y = x)

• V3 := {(0, y) | y ∈ R}

(the y-axis)

The right-hand side of the formula gives: 1 + 1 + 1 − 0 − 0 − 0 + 0 = 3, whereas the left-hand side
is dim(V1 + V2 + V3 ) = 2. So the formula does not hold for this example.
However, it does hold in every case where one of the subspaces is contained in another; hence,
without loss of generality, assume that V1 ⊆ V3 . For this situation, we have the following lemma.
Lemma: If V1 , V2 , V3 are finite-dimensional subspaces of a vector space, and V1 ⊆ V3 , then
(V1 + V2 ) ∩ V3 = (V1 ∩ V3 ) + (V2 ∩ V3 )
Proof: If v ∈ (V1 ∩V3 )+(V2 ∩V3 ), then we can write v = u +w, where u ∈ V1 ∩V3 and w ∈ V2 ∩V3 .
Because V3 is closed under addition and both u and w are in V3 , so is v. Hence, v ∈ V3 and
v = u + w, such that u ∈ V1 and w ∈ V2 . Therefore, v ∈ (V1 + V2 ) ∩ V3 . We conclude:
(V1 ∩ V3 ) + (V2 ∩ V3 ) ⊆ (V1 + V2 ) ∩ V3

(2.10)

Conversely, if v ∈ (V1 + V2 ) ∩ V3 , then we can write v = u + w, where u ∈ V1 , w ∈ V2 , and v ∈ V3 .
Since V1 ⊆ V3 (by our assumption), we can rewrite
w=

v − u .
|{z} |{z}
∈ V3

∈ V3

Since both u and v belong to V3 , so does w. Therefore, v = u + w such that u ∈ V1 ∩ V3 and
w ∈ V2 ∩ V3 . Hence, v ∈ (V1 ∩ V3 ) + (V2 ∩ V3 ). We conclude:
(V1 + V2 ) ∩ V3 ⊆ (V1 ∩ V3 ) + (V2 ∩ V3 )

(2.11)

Inclusion (2.10) and (2.11) together yield
(V1 + V2 ) ∩ V3 = (V1 ∩ V3 ) + (V2 ∩ V3 )
for V1 ⊆ V3 .
Using the standard dimension formula for the sum of two subspaces (2.43), we obtain
dim(V1 + V2 + V3 ) = dim((V1 + V2 ) + V3 )
= dim(V1 + V2 ) + dim(V3 ) − dim((V1 + V2 ) ∩ V3 )
= dim(V1 ) + dim(V2 ) + dim(V3 )

(2.12)

− dim(V1 ∩ V2 ) − dim((V1 + V2 ) ∩ V3 )
Using the assumption that V1 ⊆ V3 , we calculate:
dim((V1 + V2 ) ∩ V3 ) = dim((V1 ∩ V3 ) + (V2 ∩ V3 ))
= dim(V1 ∩ V3 ) + dim(V2 ∩ V3 ) − dim((V1 ∩ V3 ) ∩ (V2 ∩ V3 ))

If we plug in this result in the first formula (2.12), we have
dim(V1 + V2 + V3 ) = dim(V1 ) + dim(V2 ) + dim(V3 ) − dim(V1 ∩ V2 ) − dim((V1 + V2 ) ∩ V3 )
= dim(V1 ) + dim(V2 ) + dim(V3 ) − dim(V1 ∩ V2 )

= dim(V1 ) + dim(V2 ) + dim(V3 )
− dim(V1 ∩ V2 ) − dim(V1 ∩ V3 ) − dim(V2 ∩ V3 )
+ dim(V1 ∩ V2 ∩ V3 ).
So, under certain conditions (i.e. V1 ⊆ V3 ), the formula is true.
21 Show that for a finite-dimensional vector space V with subspaces V1 , V2 , V3 the following

Dimension (Section 2C)

formula holds:
dim(V1 + V2 + V3 ) = dim(V1 ) + dim(V2 ) + dim(V3 )

−

 3


dim (V1 + V2 ) ∩ V3 + dim (V1 + V3 ) ∩ V2 + dim (V2 + V3 ) ∩ V1
−
3
Solution: Recall that for any two subspaces U and W of a finite-dimensional vector space we
have
dim(U + W ) = dim U + dim W − dim(U ∩ W ).
So we can use associativity and commutativity of the sum V1 + V2 + V3 . In particular:
V1 + V2 + V3 = (V1 + V2 ) + V3
= V1 + (V2 + V3 )
= (V1 + V3 ) + V2
dim((V1 + V2 ) + V3 ) = dim(V1 + V2 ) + dim(V3 ) − dim((V1 + V2 ) ∩ V3 )
= dim V1 + dim V2 + dim V3 − dim(V1 ∩ V2 ) − dim((V1 + V2 ) ∩ V3 )
dim(V1 + (V2 + V3 )) = dim(V2 + V3 ) + dim(V1 ) − dim((V2 + V3 ) ∩ V1 )
= dim V1 + dim V2 + dim V3 − dim(V2 ∩ V3 ) − dim((V2 + V3 ) ∩ V1 )
dim((V1 + V3 ) + V2 ) = dim(V1 + V3 ) + dim(V2 ) − dim((V1 + V3 ) ∩ V2 )
= dim V1 + dim V2 + dim V3 − dim(V1 ∩ V3 ) − dim((V1 + V3 ) ∩ V2 )
Therefore, adding the three identities yields
3 dim(V1 + V2 + V3 ) = dim V1 + dim V2 + dim V3 − dim(V1 ∩ V2 ) − dim((V1 + V2 ) ∩ V3 )
+ dim V1 + dim V2 + dim V3 − dim(V2 ∩ V3 ) − dim((V2 + V3 ) ∩ V1 )
+ dim V1 + dim V2 + dim V3 − dim(V1 ∩ V3 ) − dim((V1 + V3 ) ∩ V2 )
= 3 dim V1 + 3 dim V2 + 3 dim V3
− dim(V1 ∩ V2 ) − dim(V2 ∩ V3 ) − dim(V1 ∩ V3 )
− dim((V2 + V3 ) ∩ V1 ) − dim((V1 + V3 ) ∩ V2 ) − dim((V2 + V3 ) ∩ V1 )
Dividing both sides by 3 gives the formula in question.

Linear Maps (Chapter 3)
Vector Space of Linear Maps (Section 3A)
Definition and Examples of Linear Maps (Sub-Section 3A1)
Def 3.1 (linear map):
A linear mapfrom V to W is a function T : V → W with the following properties:
• additivity: T (u + v) = T u + T v

∀u, v ∈ V

• homogeneity: T (λv) = λ(T v)

∀λ ∈ F , ∀v ∈ V

Def 3.2 (notation L(V , W ), L(V )):
The set of all linear maps from V to W is denoted by L(V , W ). For the set of all linear maps from V
to itself, we use L(V ) :≡ L(V , V ).
Thm 3.4 (linear map lemma):
Suppose v1 , . . . , vn is a basis of V and w1 , . . . , wn ∈ W , where the w’s do not have any specific properties.
Then there exists a unique linear map T ∈ L(V , W ) such that
T vk = wk

∀k ∈ {1, . . . , n}.

It is of the form
T (c1 v1 + · · · + cn vn ) = c1 w1 + · · · + cn wn .
Proof: Since the list v1 , . . . , vn is a basis of V , every v ∈ V has a unique expansion
v = c1 v1 + · · · + cn vn ,
where c1 , . . . , cn ∈ F . Define T : V → W by
T (c1 v1 + · · · + cn vn ) := c1 w1 + · · · + cn wn .
By uniqueness of the basis expansion, this rule defines a well-defined function T : V → W (otherwise a single v could receive different images). In particular, choosing v = vk (so ck = 1, ci = 0
for i ̸= k) gives
T (vk ) = wk .
Next, we verify that T is linear. Let u = a1 v1 + · · · + an vn ∈ V and v = b1 v1 + · · · + bn vn ∈ V , and let
λ ∈ F . Then,
T (u + v) = T ((a1 + b1 )v1 + · · · + (an + bn )vn )
= (a1 + b1 )w1 + · · · + (an + bn )wn
= (a1 w1 + · · · + an wn ) + (b1 w1 + · · · + bn wn )
= T (u) + T (v),
and similarly,
T (λv) = T (λb1 v1 + · · · + λbn vn )
= λb1 w1 + · · · + λbn wn
= λ(b1 w1 + · · · + bn wn )
= λT (v).
Hence, T is linear, so T ∈ L(V , W ).
Finally, to prove uniqueness, suppose S ∈ L(V , W ) is any linear map such that S(vk ) = wk for
22

Vector Space of Linear Maps (Section 3A)

each k = 1, . . . , n. Then, for any v = c1 v1 + · · · + cn vn ∈ V , we have
S(v) = c1 S(v1 ) + · · · + cn S(vn )
= c 1 w1 + · · · + c n wn
= T (v),
so S = T . Therefore, the unique linear map sending each vk to wk (for k ∈ {1, . . . , n}) is T .
Def 3.5 (addition and scalar multiplication on L(V , W )):
Suppose S, T ∈ L(V , W ) and λ ∈ F .
The sum S + T ∈ L(V , W ) and the product λT ∈ L(V , W ) are defined by:
• (S + T )(v) := Sv + T v
• (λT )(v) := λ(T v)

∀v ∈ V

∀v ∈ V

Algebraic Operations on L(V , W ) (Sub-Section 3A2)
Thm 3.6 (L(V , W )):
With these operations of addition and scalar multiplication as defined above, L(V , W ) is a vector
space.
Def 3.7:
If T ∈ L (U , V ) and S ∈ L (V , W ), then the “product” ST ∈ L (U , W ) is defined by
(ST )(u) := S(T u)

∀u ∈ U .

Thus, ST is just the usual composition S ◦ T of two functions. But with linear maps, we have distributive and associative properties (see next result), so the notation seems natural.
Thm 3.8 (algebraic properties of products of linear maps):
With these definitions, we have:
• associativity: (T1 T2 )T3 = T1 (T2 T3 ), whenever T3 maps into the domain of T2 and T2 maps into
the domain of T1 .
• identity: T I = IT = T for T ∈ L(V , W ) (The first I is the identity operator on V and the second
I is the identity operator on W . We could also write T IV = IW T ).
• distributive properties: For T , T1 , T2 ∈ L (U , V ) and S, S1 , S2 ∈ L (V , W ):
(S1 + S2 )T = S1 T + S2 T and S(T1 + T2 ) = ST1 + ST2 .
• non-commutative: ST ̸= T S in general.
Thm 3.10 (linear maps take 0 to 0):
T ∈ L(V , W ) =⇒ T (0) = 0.
Proof: T (0) = T (0 + 0) = T (0) + T (0). Now subtract T (0) on both sides.

Exercises about Vector Space of Linear Maps 3A
7

Show that every linear map from a one-dimensional vector space to itself is a multiplication by
some scalar. More precisely, prove that if dim V = 1 and T ∈ L(V ), then there exists λinF such
that T v = λv for all v ∈ V .
Proof: If T = 0, take λ = 0 and there’s nothing more to show. Otherwise, because T ̸= 0 there
exists v1 ∈ V with T (v1 ) ̸= 0. Since dim V = 1, {v1 } is a basis of V , so every v ∈ V can be written
uniquely as v = a1 v1 , a1 ∈ F . Because T (v1 ) lies in the one-dimensional space V = span(v1 ),
23

Vector Space of Linear Maps (Section 3A)

there is a unique scalar λ ∈ F such that
T (v1 ) = λv1 .
Then for any v = a1 v1 ,
T (v) = T (a1 v1 ) = a1 T (v1 ) = a1 (λv1 ) = λ(a1 v1 ) = λv
Hence, T acts by multiplication by λ on all of V , as desired.

Null Spaces and Ranges (Section 3B)

Null Spaces and Ranges (Section 3B)
Null Space and Injectivity (Sub-Section 3B1)
Def 3.11 (null space, null T ):
null T :≡ ker T :≡ {v ∈ V | T v = 0} ⊆ V for T ∈ L(V , W ).
Thm 3.13 (null space is a subspace):
For T = L(V , W ), null T is a subspace of V .
Proof: We have that 0 ∈ null T , because T (0) = 0. Suppose u, v ∈ null T and λ ∈ F . Then T (u + v) =
T u + T v = 0 + 0 = 0. Hence, u + v ∈ null T . Similarly, T (λu) = λT u = λ0 = 0, so λu ∈ null T .
Def 3.14 (injectivity):
A function T : V → W is called “injective” if
T u = T v =⇒ u = v

∀u, v ∈ V .

Or analogously:
u ̸= v =⇒ T u ̸= T v

∀u, v ∈ V .

Thm 3.15 (injectivity and null space property):
Let T ∈ L(V , W ). Then
T is injective ⇐⇒ null T = {0}.
Proof: ⇒ direction: First, assume that T is injective. Since null T is a subspace of V (by 3.13), we
have {0} ⊆ null T . To prove that null T ⊆ {0}, suppose v ∈ null T . Consequently,
T (v) = 0.
Moreover, since linear maps send 0 to 0 (by 3.10), we have
T (0) = 0.
Since T is injective, both equations imply that v = 0. Thus, we can conclude that null T = {0}, as
desired.
⇐ direction: Second, assume null T = {0}. Let u, v ∈ V such that T u = T v. Then
0 = T u − T v = T (u − v).
Thus, u − v ∈ null T = {0}, so u − v = 0, which gives u = v. Therefore, T is injective.

Range and Surjectivity (Sub-Section 3B2)
Def 3.16 (range):
range T := {T v | v ∈ V } ⊆ W for T ∈ L(V , W ).
Thm 3.18 (the range is a subspace):
If T ∈ L(V , W ), then range T is a subspace of W .
Proof: Let T ∈ L(V , W ). By 3.10, T (0) = 0, so 0 ∈ range T . Thus, the additive identity 0 is part of
range T . If w1 , w2 ∈ range T , then there exist some v1 , v2 ∈ V such that T v1 = w1 and T v2 = w2 ,
for which we have w1 + w2 = T v1 + T v2 = T (v1 + v2 ) ∈ range T . Hence, range T is closed under
addition. If w ∈ range T and λ ∈ F , then there exists a v ∈ V such that w = T v. For w it holds
that λw = λT v = T (λv) ∈ range T . Therefore, range T is closed under scalar multiplication. By
1.34, range T is a subspace of W , since we have checked for all 3 conditions.

26

Null Spaces and Ranges (Section 3B)

Def 3.19 (surjectivity):
If range T = W , then T : V → W is called “surjective” or “onto”.

Fundamental Theorem of Linear Maps (Sub-Section 3B3)
Thm 3.21 (Fundamental Theorem of Linear Maps or “Rank Nullity Theorem”):
For T ∈ L(V , W ), where V is finite-dimensional, we have that range(T ) is also finite-dimensionaland
dim V = dim null T + dim range T .
| {z } | {z }
rank

nullity

Note that T ∈ L(V , W ) is not a typo.
Proof: Let u1 , . . . , um be a basis of null T with dim null T = m. Using 2.32, this basis, which forms a
linearly independent list inside V , can be extended to a basis of V , which looks like this:
u1 , . . . , um , v1 , . . . , vn (we have been using the fact that V is finite-dimensional.)
Thus, dim V = m + n. To complete the proof, we need to show that range T is finite-dimensional
and dim range T = n. We will do this by proving that T v1 , . . . , T vn is a basis of range T .
Let v ∈ V such that for a1 , . . . , am ∈ F and b1 , . . . , bn ∈ F :
v = (a1 u1 + · · · + am um ) + (b1 v1 + · · · + bn vn ).
If we apply T , we get T v = b1 T v1 +· · ·+bn T vn . Since we can do this for every v, this implies that
range T = span(T v1 , . . . , T vn ) and that range T is finite-dimensional.
To show that T v1 , . . . , T vn is linearly independent, suppose c1 , . . . , cn ∈ F such that
c1 T v1 + · · · + cn T vn = 0.
Then T (c1 v1 + · · · + cn vn ) = 0. Hence, c1 v1 + · · · + cn vn ∈ null T . Because u1 , . . . , um spans null T , we
can write
c1 v1 + · · · + cn vn = d1 u1 + · · · + dm um or
0 = c1 v1 + · · · + cn vn − d1 u1 − · · · − dm um ,
where the d’s are in F . This last equation implies that all the c’s and d’s are 0 because the u’s
and v’s form a basis of V and are therefore linearly independent. Thus, T v1 , . . . , T vn is linearly
independent (because all c’s are 0) and hence is a basis of range T , as desired.
Thm 3.22 (linear map to a lower-dimensional space is not injective):
Suppose V and W are finite-dimensional vector spaces such that dim V > dim W . Then no linear map
from V to W is injective.
Proof: Let T ∈ L(V , W ) such that dim V > dim W . Using 3.21, we have
dim null T = dim V − dim range T
≥ dim V − dim W
> 0.
The first inequality follows from 2.37, because range T is a subspace of W . The second inequality holds because we assumed dim V > dim W . Now, the equation tells us that dim null T > 0,
implying null T is not equal to {0} (meaning that null T contains vectors other than 0). Thus, T
is not injective by 3.15.
Thm 3.24 (linear map to a higher-dimensional space is not surjective):
Suppose V and W are finite-dimensional vector spaces such that dim V < dim W . Then no linear map
27

Null Spaces and Ranges (Section 3B)

from V to W is surjective.
Proof: Let T ∈ L(V , W ) such that dim V < dim W . Then
dim range T = dim V − dim null T
≤ dim V
< dim W .
Thus, we have dim range T < dim W . So range T ̸= W . Hence, T is not surjective by 3.19.

A homogeneous system of linear equations with more variables than equations has nonzero solutions.

Suppose S, T ∈ L(V ) are such that range S ⊆ null T . Prove that (ST )2 = 0.
Proof: For any v ∈ V , (ST )2 (v) = (ST )(ST )(v) = ST (ST (v)). Since T (v) ∈ V , we have ST (v) =
S(T (v)) ∈ range S ⊆ null T , so T (S(T (v))) = 0. Thus, we compute
(ST )2 (v) = S(T (S(T (v)))) = S(0) = 0.
Therefore, (ST )2 sends every v to 0, i.e. (ST )2 = 0.

8

Suppose T ∈ L(V , W ) is injective and v1 , . . . , vn is linearly independent in V . Prove that T v1 , . . . , T vn
is linearly independent in W .
Proof: Let a1 , . . . , an ∈ F such that a1 T v1 + · · · + an T vn = 0. By the linearity of T , we can rewrite
this equation as T (a1 v1 + · · · + an vn ) = 0. Since T is injective, and T (0) = 0 by 3.10, T (u) = 0 if
and only if u = 0. Thus, a1 v1 + · · · + an vn = 0. Because v1 , . . . , vn are linearly independent vectors
in V , this forces a1 = 0, . . . , an = 0. Hence, T v1 , . . . , T vn is a linearly independent list of vectors
in W .

9

Suppose v1 , . . . , vn spans V and T ∈ L(V , W ). Show that T v1 , . . . , T vn spans range T .
Proof: Let v = a1 v1 + · · · + an vn ∈ V , where a1 , . . . , an ∈ F . If we apply T , we get
T v = a1 T v1 + · · · + an T vn ∈ range T .
Thus, every element of T v ∈ range T can be written as a linear combination of T v1 , . . . , T vn .
Hence,
range T ⊆ span(T v1 , . . . , T vn ).
Conversely, T v1 , . . . , T vn ∈ range T and range T is a subspace of V which is closed under
addition and multiplication. Therefore, span(T v1 , . . . , T vn ) ⊆ range T and hence, range T =
span(T v1 , . . . , T vn ).

12 Suppose T is a linear map from F 4 to F 2 such that
null T :≡ {(x1 , x2 , x3 , x4 ) ∈ F | x1 = 5x2 andx3 = 7x4 }.
Prove that T is surjective.
Proof: To prove that T is surjective, we have to find the dimension of null T . We can rewrite:
null T = {(5a, a, 7b, b) | a ∈ F and b ∈ F }
= {t1 (5, 1, 0, 0) + t2 (0, 0, 7, 1) | t1 ∈ F and t2 ∈ F }.
Which shows that null T is a 2-dimensional plane in F 4 , since (5, 1, 0, 0) and (0, 0, 7, 1) are
obviously linearly independent. According to the rank-nullity theorem, we have dim F 4 =
dim null T + dim range T , and thus we derive that dim range T = 2. Theorem 2.39 states that if a
subspace of a vector space has the same dimension as the vector space itself, then the subspace
equals the whole space. In this case, it follows that range T = F 2 . Therefore, T is surjective.
13 Suppose U is a three-dimensional subspace of R8 and T is a linear map from R8 to R5 such
that
null T = U .
Prove that T is surjective.
29

Null Spaces and Ranges (Section 3B)

Proof: According to the rank-nullity theorem we have
dim R8 = dim null T + dim range T ,
or
8 = 3 + dim range T .
Thus, dim range T = 5. We also know that range T ⊆ R5 . Since a subspace of full dimension
equals the whole space itself, according to 2.39, we conclude:
range T = R5 .
Hence, T is surjective.
14 Prove that there does not exist a linear map from F 5 to F 2 whose null space equals
S := {(x1 , x2 , x3 , x4 , x5 ) ∈ F 5 : x1 = 3x2 and x3 = x4 = x5 }.
Proof: We can rewrite S as
S = {(3a, a, b, b, b) : a ∈ F and b ∈ F }
= {t1 (3, 1, 0, 0, 0) + t2 (0, 0, 1, 1, 1) : t1 ∈ F and t2 ∈ F }.
Clearly, dim S = 2. Using the rank-nullity theorem 3.21, we have for every T ∈ L(F 5 , F 2 ):
dim F 5 = dim F 2 + dim null T
Hence, dim null T = 3. Therefore, null T could never equal S.
15 Suppose there exists a linear map T ∈ L(V ) on V such that its null space null(T ) and range
range(T ) are both finite-dimensional. Prove that V is finite-dimensional.
Proof: According to the rank-nullity theorem, we have: dim V = dim range(T ) + dim null(T ).
Since both dim range T and dim null T are finte by assumption, their sum is also finite. Hence,
dim V is finite, and V is finite-dimensional.
16 Suppose V and W are both finite-dimensional. Prove that there exists an injective linear map
T ∈ L(V , W ) ⇐⇒ dim V ≤ dim W .
Proof: ⇒ direction: If there exists an injective linear map T ∈ L(V , W ), then null(T ) = {0}, so
dim null(T ) = 0. By the rank-nullity theorem:
dim V = dim range(T ) ≤ dim W ,
because range(T ) ⊆ W .
⇐ direction: Let dim V ≤ dim W . For any T ∈ L(V , W ), we know
dim null(T ) = dim V − dim range(T ) ≥ dim V − dim W .
Since dim V ≤ dim W , it follows that dim null(T ) ≥ 0, which is consistent with the existence of
an injective map T ∈ L(V , W ) where null(T ) = {0}.
Explicit Construction of T : Suppose dim V ≤ dim W . Let v1 , . . . , vn be a basis for V (where
n = dim V ), and let w1 , . . . , wm be a basis of W (where m = dim W ). Define T by T (vi ) = wi for
1 ≤ i ≤ n. Then T is injective, as null(T ) = {0}.
17 Suppose V and W are both finite-dimensional. Prove that there exists a surjective linear map
from V onto W ⇐⇒ dim V ≥ dim W .
Proof: ⇒ direction: Suppose ∃T ∈ L(V , W ) such that T is surjective. Then range(T ) = W , so

30

Null Spaces and Ranges (Section 3B)

by the rank-nullity theorem:
dim V = dim range(T ) + dim null(T )
= dim W + dim null(T ).
≥ dim W .
⇐ direction: Suppose dim V ≥ dim W . For any T ∈ L(V , W ), the rank-nullity theorem implies:
dim V = dim range(T ) + dim null(T ).
Since dim V ≥ dim W , we can select T such that range(T ) = W , ensuring surjectivity.
Explicit Construction of T :
Suppose dim V ≥ dim W . Let v1 , . . . , vn be a basis of V (where n = dim V ) and let w1 , . . . , wm
be a basis of W (where m = dim W ). Define T on V by setting T (vi ) = wi for 1 ≤ i ≤ m, and
T (vi ) = 0 for m + 1 ≤ i ≤ n. Note that we have dim V ≥ dim W , which is the same as n ≥ m. So
if n = m, then we do not set any T (vi ) to be 0 and null T = {0}. If T is constructed like this, T
is surjective, as range(T ) = W . To show that, let w ∈ W such that w = a1 w1 + · · · + am wm . We
see that: T (a1 v1 + · · · + am vm ) = a1 w1 + · · · am wm = w. Thus, for every w ∈ W , we have found a
preimage v ∈ V such that T (v) = w, proving that T is surjective.
18 Suppose V and W are finite-dimensional, and that U is a subspace of V . Prove that there exists
a T ∈ L(V , W ) such that null T = U ⇐⇒ dim U ≥ dim V − dim W .
Proof: ⇒ direction: Suppose T ∈ L(V , W ) satisfies null T = U . By the rank-nullity theorem:
dim V = dim range(T ) + dim null(T )
= dim range(T ) + dim U .
Since dim range(T ) ≤ dim W , it follows that:
dim V ≤ dim W + dim U

or equivalently,

dim U ≥ dim V − dim W .
⇐ direction: Suppose dim U ≥ dim V − dim W . By the rank-nullity theorem, for any T ∈
L(V , W ):
dim V = dim range(T ) + dim null(T ).
Setting null(T ) :≡ U , we have
dim range(T ) = dim V − dim U .
Since dim V −dim U ≤ dim W , it follows that range(T ) ⊆ W , so T ∈ L(V , W ) exists. Now we want
to show how such a T could look like.
Explicit Construction of T : Suppose dim U ≥ dim V − dim W . Let u1 , . . . , uk be a basis for U
(where k = dim U ), and extend it to a basis u1 , . . . , uk , v1 , . . . , vm for V (where m = dim V − dim U ).
Let w1 , . . . , wm be a basis for W . Define T ∈ L(V , W ) by setting T (ui ) = 0 for 1 ≤ i ≤ k, and
T (vi ) = wi for 1 ≤ i ≤ m. Then null(T ) = U and range(T ) = W .
19 Suppose W is finite-dimensional and T ∈ L(V , W ). Prove that T is injective ⇐⇒ ∃S ∈ L(W , V )
such that ST is the identity operator on V .
Proof: ⇒ direction: Let T ∈ L(V , W ) be injective. Let v1 , . . . , vm be a basis of V . We know from
exercise 10 that T v1 , . . . , T vm spans range T . We also know from exercise 9 that T v1 , . . . , T vm
is linearly independent in W and thus is a basis of range T . Let n :≡ dim W . This basis can be
extended to a basis of
(3.2)

T v1 , . . . , T vm , wm+1 , . . . , wn
31

Null Spaces and Ranges (Section 3B)

of W , given m < n (dim V < dim W ). Therefore, every w ∈ W can be written as follows:
w = a1 T v1 + · · · + am T vm + am+1 wm+1 + · · · + an wn ,
for some unique scalars a1 , . . . , am , am+1 , . . . , an ∈ F . Given the basis (3.2) of W , we can define
S ∈ L(W , V ) for any w ∈ W , letting
S(w) = S(a1 T v1 + · · · + am T vm + am+1 wm+1 + · · · + an wn ) :≡ a1 v1 + · · · + am vm
Now it is easy to see that for any v ∈ V :
ST (v) = ST (a1 v1 + · · · + am vm )
= S(a1 T v1 + · · · + am T vm )
= a1 v1 + · · · + am vm = v.
Thus, ST is the identity operator on V .
⇐ direction: Let ST be the identity operator on V . That means: ∀v ∈ V : S(T )(v) = v. Suppose
u ∈ V satisfies T (u) = 0. Then we have S(T (u)) = S(0) = 0. Since ST = I, it follows that u = 0.
We conclude that null T = {0}, hence T is injective.
20 Suppose W is finite-dimensional and T ∈ L(V , W ). Prove that T is surjective ⇐⇒ ∃S ∈ L(W , V )
such that T S is the identity operator on W .
Proof: ⇒ direction: Let T ∈ L(V , W ) such that T is surjective. Hence, range T = W . Let
w1 , . . . , wm be a basis of W and, hence, of range T as well. Since T is surjective, we have for
every j ∈ {1, . . . , m} a preimage vj ∈ V of wj such that
wj = T (vj ).
Based on this list v1 , . . . , vj which has no other special properties, we can define S ∈ L(W , V ) for
each w ∈ W as follows:
S(w) = S(a1 w1 + · · · am wm ) := a1 v1 + · · · + an vn .
Now we have ∀w ∈ W :
T S(w) = a1 T S(w1 ) + · · · + an T S(wn ) = a1 w1 + · · · + an wn = w,

(3.3)

which makes T S the identity operator on W . A different way of calculating T S would be
T S(w) = T (a1 v1 + · · · + an vn ) = a1 T v1 + · · · + an T vn = a1 w1 + · · · + an wn = w. In equation (3.3) we
were using S(wj ) = vj for 1 ≤ j ≤ m.
⇐ direction: Let T ∈ L(V , W ) and S ∈ L(W , V ) such that T S is the identity operator on W . Let
w ∈ W and v ∈ V such that v = S(w). Now we apply T on both sides and get T (v) = T (S(w)) = w.
So for any w ∈ W we found a preimage v ∈ V such that T (v) = w, which makes T surjective.
21 Suppose V is finite-dimensional, U is a subspace of V and T ∈ L(V , W ). Prove that
X :≡ {v ∈ V | T v ∈ U }
is a subspace of V and
dim X = dim{v ∈ V | T v ∈ U } = dim null T + dim(U ∩ range T ).
Proof: Let v1 , v2 ∈ X. Thus v1 , v2 ∈ V as well as T (v1 + v2 ) = T v1 + T v2 ∈ U , because U is closed
under addition. =⇒ v1 + v2 ∈ X. So X is closed under addition as well. Similarly, λv1 ∈ X
for λ ∈ F . So X is closed under scalar multiplication. We also have the additive identity 0 ∈ X
because T 0 = 0 ∈ U . Therefore, X is a subspace of V .
The formula we need to prove reminds us of the ranke nulllity theorem. We want to show that:
dim X = dim null TX + dim range TX
= dim null T + dim(U ∩ range T )
32

(3.4)

Null Spaces and Ranges (Section 3B)

The first line of the equation above is just the rank-nullity theorem of the restriction of T on X.
For the second line in formula (3.4), we have null TX = T and range TX = (U ∩ range T ). Now X
is the set of all vectors in V such that T v is in U . Because 0 ∈ U , all the vectors v ∈ V sucht that
T v = 0 are contained in X. In symbols: null T ⊆ X. Hence, null T |X = null T .
Now we want to show that range T |X ⊆ (U ∩ range T ) and (U ∩ range T ) ⊆ range T |X , which
implies
range T |X = (U ∩ range T ).
If u ∈ (U ∩ range T ), then ∃v ∈ V such that u = T v and u ∈ U . So v ∈ X because T v ∈ U . Hence
u ∈ range T |X and
(U ∩ range T ) ⊆ range T |X .
Conversely, let u ∈ range T |X . According to the definition of X, there exists a w ∈ V such that
T w ∈ U . For this w we have u = T |X (w). Since T |X ist just T on a restricted domain X ⊆ V , we
have u ∈ U and u ∈ range T , hence u ∈ (U ∩ range T ). We conclude:
range T |X ⊆ (U ∩ range T ).
Therefore, range T |X = (U ∩ range T ), which validates equation (3.4)
22

(a) Suppose U , V and W are finite-dimensional vector spaces and S ∈ L(V , W ) and T ∈
L(U , V ). Prove that dim null ST ≤ dim null S + dim null T .
Proof: Note that ST ∈ L(U , W ) and null T ⊆ null ST (this implies dim null T ≤ dim null ST ).
According to the rank-nullity theorem, the following two formulas hold:
dim U = dim range T + dim null T
dim U = dim range ST + dim null ST
Equating the two formulas, we obtain:
dim null ST = dim range T − dim range ST + dim null T

(3.5)

So, what is dim range T − dim range ST ? It equals the dimension of the null space of the
restriction map S|range T . If we can show that dim range T − dim range ST ≤ dim null S, the
proof is complete. Consider the restriction map
S|range T : range T → W .
Notice two things. First, for every x ∈ U we have S(T (x)) = (S|range T )(T (x)); hence,
range S|range T = range ST .

(3.6)

Second, null(S|range T ) = {y ∈ range T | S(y) = 0}. Clearly, every such y is contained in null S,
so
(3.7)

null S|range T ⊆ null S.

Now we can apply the Rank-Nullity Theorem to S|range T and use (3.6) and (3.7) consecutively. Thus,
dim range T = dim null S|range T + dim range S|range T
= dim null S|range T + dim range ST
≤ dim null S + dim range ST .
Rearranging, we obtain:
dim range T − dim range ST ≤ dim null S.
Therefore, (3.5) becomes
dim null ST ≤ dim null S + dim null T .
33

Null Spaces and Ranges (Section 3B)

Note: If dim range T = dim range ST , (3.5) becomes dim null ST = dim null T .
(b) Give an example of S, T ∈ L(F 5 ) with ST = 0 and dim range T S = 2.
Solution: Let S, T ∈ L(F 5 ) such that for all x ∈ F 5 :
S(x) :≡ (0, 0, 0, x4 , x5 )

and

T (x) :≡ (x1 + x4 , x2 + x5 , 0, 0, 0)

If we compute ST and T S for x ∈ F 5 we get
ST (x) = S((x1 + x4 , x2 + x5 , 0, 0, 0)) = (0, 0, 0, 0, 0) = 0
T S(x) = T ((0, 0, 0, x4 , x5 )) = (0 + x4 , 0 + x5 , 0, 0, 0) = (x4 , x5 , 0, 0, 0)
Hence, dim range T S = 2.
24 Suppose dim V = 5 and S, T ∈ L(V ) are such that ST = 0. Prove that dim range T S ≤ 2.
Proof: According to the rank-nullity theorem, we have
5 = dim range T + dim null T .
5 = dim range S + dim null S
From exercise 3B.22, we know
dim null ST ≤ dim null S + dim null T ,
5 ≤ dim null S + dim null T .
From exercise 3B.23, we know
dim range T S ≤ min{dim range S, dim range T }
= min{5 − dim null S, 5 − dim null T }

(3.8)

(3.9)

Equation (3.8) and the equation (3.9) above tell us, that range T S is at its maximum, if either
dim null S or dim null T is 2 and the other one is 3. Hence dim range T S = min{2, 1} = 2.
25 Suppose W is finite-dimensional and S, T ∈ L(V , W ). Prove that null S ⊆ null T ⇐⇒ there
exists a E ∈ L(W ) such that T = ES.
Proof: ⇒ direction: Let S, T ∈ L(V , W ) such that null S ⊆ null T . (i.e. dim null S ≤ dim null T ).
Let U be a subspace of V such that V = null S ⊕ U . This implies that
S|U : U → range S
is injective, because null S|U = {0}. Using the rank nullity-theorem and the fact that dim null S|U =
0, we can conclude that:
dim U = dim range S|U = dim range S.
The equation above also made use of the fact that range S|U = range S. This is because any
vector v ∈ V can be written as v = r + u, where r ∈ null S and u ∈ U . For this v we have:
S(v) = S(r) + S(u) = S(u) = S|U (u) = S|U (r) + S|U (u) = S|U (v).
Thus, S and S|U have the same range, range S|U = range S.
Now if u1 , . . . , un is a basis of U , then Su1 , . . . , Sun is a basis of range S ⊆ W . Therefore, we can
extend Su1 , . . . , Sun to a basis
Su1 , . . . , Sun , wn+1 , . . . , wm
of W . Note that the extension might be empty and no w’s are added. If n = m the basis of W just
consists of Su1 , . . . , Sun and range S = W . So we have n ≤ m for n = dim span(S) and m = dim W .
Now we define E : W → W for w ∈ W , letting
E(w) = E(a1 Su1 + · · · + an Sun + an+1 wn+1 + · · · + am wm ) :≡ a1 T u1 + · · · + an T un .
If we decompose v ∈ V = null S ⊕ U again as v = r + u where r ∈ null S and u ∈ U , then
ES(v) = ES(r + u) = ES(u) = E(a1 Su1 + · · · + an Sun ) = a1 T u1 + · · · + an T un .
34

Null Spaces and Ranges (Section 3B)

Since null S ⊆ null T , and we take the same v = r + u, we have T (r) = 0. Hence:
T (v) = T (r) + T (u) = T (u) = a1 T u1 + · · · + an T un .
Thus we conclude: ES = T .
⇐ direction: Assume W is finite-dimensional and we have S, T ∈ L(V , W ) and E ∈ L(W ) such
that T = ES. Now assume that null S is not a subset of null T . Then there exists a v ∈ V such
that S(v) = 0 but T (v) ̸= 0. Because of 3.10 it must be that E(0) = 0, and thus ES(v) = 0. But we
assumed that T = ES. Now we have ES ̸= T (because T (v) ̸= 0). The assumption that null S is
not a subset of null T has lead to a contradiction. Therefore, null S ⊆ null T , as desired.
26 Suppose that V is finite-dimensional and S, T ∈ L(V , W ). Prove that range S ⊆ range T ⇐⇒
there exists E ∈ L(V ) such that S = T E.
Proof: ⇒ direction: Let V be finite-dimensional and S, T ∈ L(V , W ) such that range S ⊆
range T . Let Q be a subspace of V such that V = null S ⊕ Q. Let q1 , . . . , qm be a basis of Q.
We can extend this list to a basis
q1 , . . . , qm , rm+1 , . . . , rn
of V with dim V = n. Note that rm+1 , . . . , rn is a basis of the null space of S, i.e null S. The
restriction S|Q is injective because null S|Q = {0}. This makes
Sq1 , . . . , Sqm
a basis of the range of S, i.e range S. From range S ⊆ range T , it follows that ∀j ∈ {1, . . . , m}:
∃uj ∈ V such that T (uj ) = S(qj ).
So we have a list of distinct preimage vectors u1 , . . . , um . The linear map lemma 3.4 tells us, we
can construct E : V → V as follows:
E(qk ) = uk ∀k ∈ {1, . . . , m}
E(rk ) = 0

(3.10)

∀k ∈ {m + 1, . . . , n}

This lemma guarantees the existence (and uniqueness) of a linear map on V that sends the
basis vectors q’s and r’s to the specified vectors u’s and 0’s respectively. This ensures E is well
defined. For a v ∈ V , given as v = (c1 q1 + · · · cm qm ) + (cm+1 rm+1 + · · · + cn rn ) with its coordinates
and its basis, the linear map E looks like this:
E(v) :≡ c1 u1 + · · · + cm um
One can verify that this coincides with the definition (3.10) above, using the fact that E is linear.
Now what does T E look like? It computes as follows:


T (E(v)) = T E (c1 q1 + · · · + cm qm ) + (cm+1 rm+1 + · · · + cn rn )
= T (c1 u1 + · · · + cm um ) = c1 T (u1 ) + · · · + cm T (um )
= c1 S(q1 ) + · · · + cm S(qm ).
Compare this with S:


S(v) = S (c1 q1 + · · · + cm qm ) + (cm+1 rm+1 + · · · + cn rn ) = S(c1 q1 + · · · + cm qm )
= c1 S(q1 ) + · · · + cm S(qm ).
Therefore, S = T E.
⇐ direction: Suppose there exists E ∈ L(V ) such that S = T E. Then for any v ∈ V , S(v) =
T (E(v)) implies that S(v) ∈ range T . Hence, range S ⊆ range T .
27 Suppose P ∈ L(V ) and P 2 = P . Prove that V = null P ⊕ range P .
Proof: Suppose U is a subspace of a finite-dimensional vector space V such that V = null P ⊕U .
35

Null Spaces and Ranges (Section 3B)

We want to show that U = range P . We do this by showing range P ⊆ U and U ⊆ range P .
Step 1: Let v ∈ range P . We can write v = r + u such that r ∈ null P and u ∈ U . Thus, we have
P (P (v)) = P (v) ∈ range P ,
and P (v) = P (r) + P (u) = P (u). The formula P 2 = P tells us that P acts as the identity operator
on range P . So we can just write: v = u; hence, v ∈ U . Therefore,
range P ⊆ U .
Step 2: Conversely, let u ∈ U . Notice that we can write
u = (u − P (u)) +P (u).
| {z }

(3.11)

∈ null P

u − P (u) lies in null P , because
P (u − P (u)) = P (u) − P (P (u)) = P (u) − P (u) = 0.
The decomposition of any vector in U (or V ) as an element of null P plus an element of U is
unique. Thus, the two decompositions of u ∈ U , u = 0 + u and (3.11) must coincide. This forces
u − P (u) = 0 and P (u) = u. Therefore, we conclude that u = P (u), which means that u ∈ range P .
It follows that
U ⊆ range P
Therefore, U = range P .
Alternative proof: For any v ∈ V , let u := P (v) and w := v − P (v). Then clearly, v = w + u. By
definition, u ∈ range P . If we apply P to w, we have
P (w) = P (v − P (v)) = P (v) − P (P (v)) = P (v) − P (v) = 0.
Thus, w ∈ null P .
Now we have to verify the uniqueness of the sum. Suppose a vector u lies both in null P and
range P . Then, u = P (v) for some v ∈ V and also P (u) = 0. But since P (u) = u, we have u = 0.
Therefore, we conclude that the intersection is trivial:
null P ∩ range P = {0}.
This implies,
V = null P ⊕ range P .

Def (matrix notation for operators (see later)):
For T ∈ L(V ), we use the notation
M(T , (v1 , . . . , vn )) := M(T , (v1 , . . . , vn ), (v1 , . . . , vn )).

Addition and Scalar Multiplication of Matrices (Sub-Section 3C2)
Thm 3.35 (matrix of the sum of linear maps):
M(S + T ) = M(S) + M(T ) for S, T ∈ L(V , W ).
Thm 3.38 (matrix of a scalar times a linear map):
M(λT ) = λM(T ) for λ ∈ F and T ∈ L(V , W ).
Def 3.39 (notation F m,n ):
F m,n denotes the set of all m-by-n matrices with entries in F , where m, n ∈ N.
Thm 3.40 (dimension of F m,n ):
F m,n is a vector space with dim F m,n = mn.
Proof: It is easy to verify that F m,n is a vector space. The list of length mn that consists of distinct
m-by-n matrices that have 0 in all entries except a 1 in one entry is a basis of F m,n . Hence,
dim F m,n = mn.

Thm 3.43 (matrix of product of linear maps):

Def 3.44 (notation: Aj,• , A•,k ):
Let A ∈ F m,n . Then:
• If 1 ≤ j ≤ m, then Aj,• denotes the 1-by-n matrix consisting of row j of A.
• If 1 ≤ k ≤ n, then A•,k denotes the m-by-1 matrix consisting of column k of A.
Thm 3.46 (entry of matrix product equals row times column):
If A ∈ F m,n and B ∈ F n,p , then we have for 1 ≤ j ≤ m and 1 ≤ k ≤ p:
(AB)j,k = Aj,• B•,k .

If A ∈ F m,n and B ∈ F n,p , then we have for 1 ≤ k ≤ p:
(AB)•,k = AB•,k .

Matrices (Section 3C)

Column-Row Factorization and Rank of a Matrix (Sub-Section 3C4)
Def 3.52 (column rank, row rank):
For A ∈ F m,n , the “column rank” of A is the dimension of the span of the columns of A in F m,1 . The
“row rank” of A is the dimension of the span of the rows of A in F 1,n .
Def 3.54 (transpose, At ):
The “transpose” of a matrix A ∈ F m,n , denoted by At ∈ F n,m , is obtained by interchanging rows and
columns. Its entries are given by the equation
 
At
= Aj,k ∀k ∈ {1, . . . , m}, j ∈ {1, . . . , n}.
k,j

Thm 3.56 (column-row factorization):
Suppose A ∈ F m,n with column rank c ≥ 1. Then there exist C ∈ F m,c and R ∈ F c,n such that
A = CR.
Thm 3.57 (column rank and row rank):
If A ∈ F m,n , then column rank of A = row rank of A. This leads to the next definition.
Def 3.58 (rank):
The “rank” of a matrix A ∈ F m,n is the column rank of A.

Exercises about Matrices 3C

41

Invertibility and Isomorphisms (Section 3D)

Invertibility and Isomorphisms (Section 3D)
Invertible Linear Maps (Sub-Section 3D1)
Def 3.59 (invertible, inverse):
S ∈ L(W , V ) with ST = IV and T S = IW is called the “inverse” of the “invertible” linear map T ∈
L(V , W ).
Thm 3.60 (inverse is unique):
An invertible linear map has a unique inverse.
Proof: Suppose T ∈ L(V , W ) is invertible and S1 and S2 are inverses of T . Hence,
S1 = S1 I = S1 (T S2 ) = (S1 T )S2 = IS2 = S2 .
Therefore, S1 = S2 .
Def 3.61 (notation T −1 ):
Since the inverse is unique, the inverse is denoted by T −1 ∈ L(W , V ) for T ∈ L(V , W ). We have
T −1 T = IV and T T −1 = IW .
Thm 3.62 (invertibility, injectivity and surjectivity):
A linear map is invertible ⇐⇒ it is injective and surjective.
Thm 3.65 (injectivity is equivalent to surjectivity):
For T ∈ L(V , W ), if V and W are finite-dimensional, injectivity is equivalent to surjectivity:
T is invertible ⇐⇒ T is injective ⇐⇒ T is surjective.
Equivalently:
T is not invertible ⇐⇒ T is not injective ⇐⇒ T is not surjective.
Proof: The rank-nullity theorem (3.21) or fundamental theorem of linear maps states that
dim V = dim null T + dim range T .

(3.12)

If T is injective, dim null T = 0, and therefore
dim range T = dim V = dim W ,
which means T is surjective (range T = dim W ). This is because every linearly independent list
of vectors of length dim W is a basis by 2.38. And that’s how dimension is defined. (TODO)
If T is surjective, we have dim range T = dim W to begin with, and therefore (3.12) becomes
dim null T = dim V − dim range T = dim V − dim W = 0,
which makes T also injective. Since injectivity and surjectivity together imply invertibility, this
ends the proof.
Thm 3.68 (ST = I ⇐⇒ T S = I on vector spaces of the same dimension):
Suppose V and W are finite-dimensional vector spaces of the same dimension. Let S ∈ L(W , V ) and
T ∈ L(V , W ). Then ST = I ⇐⇒ T S = I.
Proof: ⇒ direction: Let S ∈ L(W , V ) and T ∈ L(V , W ) such that ST = I. If v ∈ V and T v = 0, then
v = Iv = (ST )v = S(T v) = S(0) = 0.
Thus, null T = {0}, and therefore, T is injective by 3.15. Because V and W have the same
dimension, T is invertible by 3.65. Now, multiply both sides of the equation ST = I by T −1 to

42

Invertibility and Isomorphisms (Section 3D)

conclude:
S = T −1 .
Hence, T S = T T −1 = I, as desired.
⇐ direction: Right above, we have shown that ST = I implies that T S = I. To prove the
implication in the other direction, simply reverse the roles of S and T and V and W , showing
that T S = I implies that ST = I.

Isomorphic Vector Spaces (Sub-Section 3D2)
Def 3.69:
An isomorphism is an invertible linear map. Two vector spaces are called isomorphic if there is an
isomorphism from one vector space to the other, T : V → W .
Thm 3.70 (dimension shows whether vector spaces are isomorphic):
Two finite-dimensionalvector spaces U , V over F are isomorphic ⇐⇒ they have the same dimension
(i.e., dim U = dim V ).
Proof: exercise
⇒ direction: rank-nullity theorem
⇐ direction: surjectivity and injectivity.
Thm 3.71 (L(V , W ) and F m,n are isomorphic):
Suppose v1 , . . . , vn is a basis of V and w1 , . . . , wm is a basis of W . Then M is an isomorphism between
L(V , W ) and F m,n .
Proof: exercise (injectivity and surjectivity)
Thm 3.72 (dim L(V , W ) = (dim V ) · (dim W )):
Suppose V , W are finite-dimensional. Then L(V , W ) is finite-dimensional, and
dim L(V , W ) = (dim V ) · (dim W ).
Proof: Suppose v1 , . . . , vn is a basis of V and w1 , . . . , wm is a basis of W . By 3.71, there is an isomorphism between L (V , W ) and F m,n , and thus, they have the same dimension. Now, 3.40 tells us
that dim F m,n = mn; therefore,
dim L (V , W ) = dim F m,n = m · n = (dim V ) · (dim W ),
as desired.

Thm 3.75 (M(T )•,k = M(T vk )):
Suppose T ∈ L(V , W ), v1 , . . . , vn is a basis of V , and w1 , . . . , wm is a basis of W . Let 1 ≤ k ≤ n. Then the

43

Invertibility and Isomorphisms (Section 3D)

k th column of M(T ), which is denoted by M(T )•,k , equals M(T vk ):
M(T )•,k = M(T vk )

∀k ∈ {1, . . . , n}.

Thm 3.76 (linear maps act like matrix multiplication):
Suppose T ∈ L(V , W ) and v ∈ V . Suppose v1 , . . . , vn is a basis of V and w1 , . . . , wm is a basis of W . Then
M(T v) = M(T ) · M(v).
Or using different notation:
M(T v, (v1 , . . . , vn ), (w1 , . . . , wm )) = M(T , (v1 , . . . , vn ), (w1 , . . . , wm )) · M(v).
Thm 3.78 (dimension of range T and column rank of M(T )):
Suppose dim V < ∞ and dim W < ∞. Then, for T ∈ L(V , W ):
dim range T = column rank of M(T ).

Change of basis (Sub-Section 3D4)
Def 3.80 (invertible, inverse, A−1 ):
A square matrix A is called invertible if there is some square matrix B of the same size such that
AB = BA = I.
We call B the inverse of A, denoted by A−1 :≡ B. Rules:
• (A−1 )−1 = A
• (AC)−1 = C −1 A−1 (Because (AC)(C −1 A−1 ) = I and (C −1 A−1 )(AC) = I)
Thm 3.81 (matrix of product of linear maps):
Suppose T ∈ L(U , V ) and S ∈ L(V , W ). If u1 , . . . , um is a basis of U , v1 , . . . , vn is a basis of V , and
w1 , . . . , wp is a basis of W . Note that dim U = m, dim V = n, dim W = p. Then we have:
M(ST , (u1 , . . . , um ), (w1 , . . . , wp )) =
M(S, (v1 , . . . , vn ), (w1 , . . . , wp )) · M(T , (u1 , . . . , um ), (v1 , . . . , vn )).
Or using different notation:
M(ST ) = M(S) · M(T ).
Proof: This is just another way of writing 3.43.
Thm 3.82 (matrix of identity operator with respect to two bases):
Suppose that u1 , . . . , um and v1 , . . . , vm are bases of V . Then the matrices

Proof: Careful application of 3.81 yields

Now, interchange the roles of the u’s and the v’s, getting

So, the product of both matrices equals In , which is the identity in F n,n .

Thm 3.86:
If v1 , . . . , vm is a basis of V and T ∈ LV is invertible, then
M(T −1 ) = (M(T ))−1 ,

Exercises about Invertibility and Isomorphisms 3D
1

Suppose T ∈ L(V , W ) is invertible. Show that T −1 is invertible and (T −1 )−1 = T .
Proof: T −1 is the unique element in L(W , V ) such that
T −1 T = IV and T T −1 = IW .
But this makes T the unique element in L(V , W ) such that
T (T −1 ) = IW and T −1 T = IV .
This makes T the inverse of T −1 . Since the inverse is unique by THM 3.60, we conclude that
(T −1 ) = T .

2

Suppose T ∈ L(U , V ) and S ∈ L(V , W ) are both invertible linear maps. Prove that ST ∈ L(U , W )
is invertible and that (ST )−1 = T −1 S −1 .
Proof: Let T ∈ L(U , V ) and S ∈ L(V , W ) be both invertible linear maps. Thus:
(ST )−1 (T −1 S −1 ) = S(T T −1 )S −1 = SS −1 = IW
(T −1 S −1 )(ST )−1 = T −1 (S −1 S)T = T −1 T = IU
Therefore, (ST )−1 = T −1 S −1 .

3

Suppose V is finite-dimensional and T ∈ L(V ). Prove that the following are equivalent:
(a) T is invertible.
(b) T v1 , . . . , T vn is a basis of V for every basis v1 , . . . , vn of V .
(c) T v1 , . . . , T vn is a basis of V for some basis v1 , . . . , vn of V .
Proof: For all proof-steps, let T ∈ L(V ) and V be a finite-dimensional vector space. In each
step of the proof, we show one equivalence listed above.
Step 1: Clearly, (b) implies (c).
Step 2: We start by showing that (c) implies (a). Actually, the proof that (b) implies (a) would
look the same. Suppose T v1 , . . . , T vn is a basis of V for some basis v1 , . . . , vn . Since dim V = n,
we have range T = V . It follows that T v1 , . . . , T vn is a basis of range T . So T is a surjective map
on V by the definition 3.19 of surjectivity. Using theorem 3.65, we conclude that T is invertible. It states that for a linear map from a vector space to another one of the same dimension,
invertibility, injectivity and surjectivity are equivalent.
Step 3: Now we want to show that (c) implies (b). So let T v1 , . . . , T vn be a basis of V for some
basis of v1 , . . . , vn of V . Let u1 , . . . , un be another basis of V . We already know that (c) =⇒
45

Invertibility and Isomorphisms (Section 3D)

(a), so T is injective. This means, that T u1 , . . . , T un is a basis of V as well. Since u1 , . . . , un was
arbitrary, we have shown that (c) =⇒ (b).
Step 4: Lastly, we want to show that (a) implies (c). Suppose T ∈ L(V ) such that T is invertible.
Let v1 , . . . , vn be a basis of V . Let a1 , . . . , an ∈ F such that
0 = a1 T v1 + · · · an T vn
Hence,
T −1 (0) = T −1 (a1 T v1 + · · · an T vn ) ⇐⇒
0 = a1 T −1 T v1 + · · · + an T −1 T vn ⇐⇒
0 = a1 v1 + · · · an vn .
Therefore, 0 = a1 = · · · = an , because all the v’s are linearly independent. We conclude that
T v1 , · · · , T vn is a linearly independent list of length n = dim V and hence a basis of V . Thus, (a)
implies (c).
Overall, we have shown that (b) =⇒ (c), (c) =⇒ (b), (c) =⇒ (a), and (a) =⇒ (c). So all three
statements are equivalent.
5

Suppose V is finite-dimensional, U is a subspace of V , and S ∈ L(U , V ). Prove that there exists
an invertible linear map T ∈ L(U , V ) such that T u = Su ∀u ∈ U ⇐⇒ S is injective.
Proof: Suppose V is finite-dimensional, U is a subspace of V and S ∈ L(U , V ).
⇒ direction: Suppose for every u ∈ U we have that T u = Su. Let u1 , u2 ∈ U such that S(u1 ) =
S(u2 ). This implies that
T (u1 ) = T (u2 )
T −1 T (u1 ) = T −1 T (u2 )
u1 = u2
Hence, S is injective.
⇐ direction: Suppose S is injective. Let u1 , . . . , um be a basis of U and Su1 , . . . , Sum be a basis
of range S. We extend
(3.13)

u1 , . . . , um , vm+1 , . . . , vm
to a basis of V , and

(3.14)

Su1 , . . . , Sum , rm+1 , . . . , rn
to a basis of V as well. We define T ∈ L(V ) for each v ∈ V as follows:
T (v) = T (c1 u1 + · · · + cm um + cm+1 vm+1 + · · · + cn vn )
:= c1 Su1 + · · · + cm Sum + cm+1 rm+1 + · · · + cn rn .

Clearly, ∀u ∈ U : T (u) = S(u). Now we have to show that T is injective and surjective. If
v1 , v2 ∈ V such that
y1 = T (v1 ) = T (v2 ) = y2
All the c coefficents of y1 and y2 , which are defined over the basis (3.14) match the c coefficients
of v1 and v2 , which are defined over the basis (3.13). So these representations are unique and
therefore v1 = v2 . We conclude that T is injective. To prove that T is surjective, we have to show
that for any given y ∈ V there is a v ∈ V such that T (v) = y. So let
y := c1 Su1 + · · · + cm Sum + cm+1 rm+1 + · · · + cn rn .
Then there is exactly one v ∈ V such that T (v) = y, namely
v := c1 u1 + · · · + cm um + cm+1 vm+1 + · · · + cn vn .
For this choice of y and v, we have T (v) = y. Hence, T is injective and surjective. This makes T
46

Invertibility and Isomorphisms (Section 3D)

invertible.

Products and Quotients of Vector Spaces (Section 3E)
Products of Vector Spaces (Sub-Section 3E1)
Def 3.87 (product of vector spaces):
The product, addition, and scalar multiplication of a list of vector spaces V1 , . . . , Vm are defined as
follows:
V1 × · · · × Vm :≡ {(v1 , . . . , vm ) | v1 ∈ V1 , . . . , vm ∈ Vm }
(u1 , . . . , um ) + (v1 , . . . , vm ) :≡ (u1 + v1 , . . . , um + vm )
λ(v1 , . . . , vm ) :≡ (λv1 , . . . , λvm )
Thm 3.89 (product of vector space is a vector space):
V1 × · · · × Vm together with addition and scalar multiplication is a vector space over F .
Thm 3.92 (dimension of a product is the sum of dimensions):
Suppose V1 , . . . , Vm are finite-dimensional vector spaces. Then V1 × · · · × Vm is finite-dimensional, and
dim(V1 × · · · × Vm ) = dim V1 + · · · + dim Vm .

Thm 3.93 (products and direct sums):
Let Γ : V1 × · · · × Vm → V1 + · · · + Vm such that
Γ(v1 , . . . , vm ) = v1 + · · · + vm

∀v1 ∈ V1 , . . . , vm ∈ Vm .

Then
V1 + · · · + Vm is a direct sum ⇐⇒ Γ is injective/invertible.
Proof: By 3.15, Γ is injective ⇐⇒ v1 = · · · = vm = 0 [Stated correctly, Γ is injective ⇐⇒ null Γ = {⃗0}].
This means the only way to write 0 as a sum v1 + · · · + vm , where each vk ∈ Vk , is by taking each
vk = 0. Thus, 1.45 shows that Γ is injective ⇐⇒ V1 + · · · + Vm is a direct sum, as desired.

codomain ⊆ V

dim(V1 + · · · + Vm ) = dim(V1 × · · · × Vm ).
We can use 3.92 to conclude that
dim(V1 × · · · × Vm ) = dim V1 + · · · + dim Vm and therefore
dim(V1 + · · · + Vm ) = dim V1 + · · · + dim Vm .
Since Γ is injective, 3.93 tells us that V1 + · · · + Vm is a direct sum. So, we have that
dim(V1 + · · · + Vm ) = dim V1 + · · · + dim Vm
if and only if V1 + · · · + Vm is a direct sum.
Special case for m=2: For the special case where m = 2, we can use 2.37, where
dim(V1 + V2 ) = dim V1 + dim V2 − dim(V1 ∩ V2 ),
and we get dim(V1 ∩ V2 ) = 0 ⇐⇒ V1 + V2 = V1 ⊕ V2 by 2.43.

Quotient Spaces (Sub-Section 3E2)
Def 3.95 (notation v + U ):
v + U :≡ {v + u | u ∈ U } for v ∈ V and U ⊆ V .
Def 3.97 (translate):
The set v + U is said to be a “translate” of U , where v ∈ V and U ⊆ V .
Def 3.99 (quotient space, V /U ):
Suppose U is a subspace of V . Then the “quotient space” V /U is the set of all translates of U . Thus,
V /U :≡ {v + U | v ∈ V }.
Example 3.100:
If U = {(x, 2x) ∈ R2 | x ∈ R} =⇒ R2 /U is the set of all lines with slope 2.
If U is a plane in R3 =⇒ R3 /U is the set of all planes parallel to U .
Thm 3.101 (two translates of a subspace are equal or disjoint):
If U is a subspace of V and v, w ∈ V (not a typo!), then
v − w ∈ U ⇐⇒ v + U = w + U ⇐⇒ (v + U ) ∩ (w + U ) ̸= ∅.
Proof: Step 1: First, suppose v − w ∈ U . If u ∈ U , then


v + u = (w − w + v + u) = w + (v − w) +u , thus v + u ∈ w + U .
| {z }
∈U

Similarly, because v − w ∈ U , we also have w − v ∈ U , and thus,


w + u = (v − v + w + u) = v + (w − v) +u , hence w + u ∈ v + U .
| {z }
∈U

We have shown that ∀u ∈ U : v + u ∈ w + U , which is equivalent to
v +U ⊆ w+U
and ∀u ∈ U : w + u ∈ v + U , which is equivalent to
w + U ⊆ v + U.
This implies v + U = w + U , given that v − w ∈ U , which proves the first equivalence.
Step 2: The equation v + U = w + U implies that (v + U ) ∩ (w + U ) ̸= ∅.
49

Products and Quotients of Vector Spaces (Section 3E)

Step 3: Now, suppose (v + U ) ∩ (w + U ) ̸= ∅. Thus, there exist u1 , u2 ∈ U such that
v + u1 = w + u2 ,
Therefore, v − w = u2 − u1 . Hence, v − w ∈ U .
Def 3.102 (addition and scalar multiplication on V /U ):
Suppose U is a subspace of V . Then “addition” and “scalar multiplication” are defined on V /U by
(v + U ) + (w + U ) :≡ (v + w) + U
λ(v + U ) :≡ (λv) + U
∀v, w ∈ V and ∀λ ∈ F .
Thm 3.103 (quotient space is a vector space):
V /U is a vector space with additive identity 0 + U , which is equal to U , and the additive inverse
(−v) + U .
Def 3.104 (quotient map, π):
Suppose U ⊆ V . The “quotient map” π : V → V /U is the linear map defined by
π(v) = v + U

∀v ∈ V .

Thm 3.105:
Suppose dim V < ∞ and U ⊆ V .
=⇒ dim V /U = dim V − dim U .
Def 3.106 (notation Te):
Suppose T ∈ L(V , W ). Define Te : V /(null T ) → W by
Te(v + null T ) = T v.
Thm 3.107 (null space and range of Te):
Suppose T ∈ L(V , W ). Then:
(a) Te ◦ π = T , where π is the quotient map V onto V /(null T );
(b) Te is injective;
(c) range Te = range T ;
(d) V /(null T ) and range T are isomorphic vector spaces.

Exercises about Null Spaces and Ranges 3E
6

Suppose that v, x ∈ V and that U , W are subspaces of V such that v + U = x + W . Prove that
U = W . Proof: Since v ∈ v + U and v + U = x + W , it follows that v ∈ x + W . Hence, there is
w0 ∈ W with v = x + w0 , so (x − v) = −w0 ∈ W . Now,
U = (−v) + (v + U ) = (−v) + (x + W ) = (x − v) + W = W ,
where the last equality uses x − v ∈ W and the fact that translating a subspace by one of its own
elements leaves it unchanged (i.e., (x − v) + W = W ). Hence, U = W .

Proof: By the definition of intersection, we get
A1 ∩ A2 = {x ∈ V : x = v + u1 and x = w + u2 , u1 ∈ U1 , u2 ∈ U2 }.

Dual Space and Dual Map (Sub-Section 3F1)
Def 3.108:
A “linear functional” φ is an element of L(V , F ). So, φ ∈ L(V , F ).
Examples:
